Option D groups the first three Cys residues (10, 46, 79) together and the last three (150, 154, 157) together. This makes biological sense because:

2. The first three cysteines (10, 46, 79) are more spread out in the linear sequence but could be brought together in 3D space by protein folding. The spacing is C-X35-C-X32-C.

This pattern is reminiscent of the radical SAM enzyme superfamily, which has a characteristic C-X3-C-X2-C motif for the [4Fe-4S] cluster that binds SAM (S-adenosylmethionine). 

Actually, let me reconsider. In radical SAM enzymes, there are typically two [4Fe-4S] clusters:
- The &quot;radical SAM&quot; cluster (also called the auxiliary cluster or SAM-binding cluster) with the characteristic C-X3-C-X2-C motif
- The &quot;electron transfer&quot; cluster (sometimes called the &quot;dissociable&quot; cluster or &quot;cysteine-rich&quot; cluster)

The radical SAM cluster typically has the motif C-X3-C-X2-C. Looking at positions 150, 154, 157:
- 150 to 154: C(150)-D(151)-R(152)-F(153)-C(154) = C-X3-C
- 154 to 157: C(154)-T(155)-Y(156)-C(157) = C-X3-C

For the electron transfer cluster, the first three Cys residues (10, 46, 79) could serve as three of the four ligands. The fourth ligand might be elsewhere (possibly another Cys or even a different residue).

This matches option D: Cluster 1: residues 10, 46, and 79; cluster 2: residues 150, 154, and 157.

The spacing pattern makes sense:
- Cluster 1 (electron transfer cluster): C(10)...C(46)...C(79) - spread out in sequence, brought together by folding
- Cluster 2 (radical SAM/SAM-binding cluster): C(150)X3C(154)X3C(157) - closely spaced, characteristic of radical SAM enzymes

This is the most logical grouping. The answer is D.
